The reverse Burnett conjecture for null dusts
Arthur Touati

Abstract
Given a generic solution 𝐠 0 \mathbf{g}_{0} of the Einstein-null dusts system without restriction on the number of dusts, we construct families of solutions ( 𝐠 λ ) λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] (\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]} of the Einstein vacuum equations such that 𝐠 λ − 𝐠 0 \mathbf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathbf{g}_{0} and ∂ ( 𝐠 λ − 𝐠 0 ) \partial(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathbf{g}_{0}) converges respectively strongly and weakly to 0 when λ → 0 \lambda\to 0 . Our construction, based on a multiphase geometric optics ansatz, thus extends the validity of the reverse Burnett conjecture without symmetry to a large class of massless kinetic spacetimes. In order to deal with the finite but arbitrary number of direction of oscillations we work in a generalised wave gauge and control precisely the self-interaction of each wave but also the interaction of waves propagating in different null directions, relying crucially on the non-linear structure of the Einstein vacuum equations. We also provide the construction of oscillating initial data solving the vacuum constraint equations and which are consistent with the spacetime ansatz.



1
Introduction

In this article we construct oscillatory solutions of the Einstein vacuum equations

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g})=0,

(1.1)

where
\mathbf{g}
is a Lorentzian metric on a 4-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{M}
and
R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g})
is the Ricci tensor of
\mathbf{g}
. These solutions, denoted
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
, depend on a small wavelength
\lambda>0
and oscillate at frequency
\lambda^{-1}
. We are particularly interested in their behaviour in the high-frequency limit
\lambda\to 0
and our construction, based on a geometric optics expansion of the solutions, seems to be the first to allow a finite but arbitrary number of directions of oscillation with no symmetry assumption. It builds on previous works by the author
[
Tou23a
,
Tou23b
]
, where only one direction of oscillation was allowed. It is motivated by Burnett’s conjecture in general relativity and by the study of high-frequency gravitational waves initiated by Choquet-Bruhat. We discuss further these two aspects in Sections
1.1
and
1.2
below where we also give two rough versions of our main result (see Theorem
1.3
for the precise version) from these two perspectives.

1.1
The Burnett conjecture

The first motivation of our work is the description of the closure of the set of solutions to (
1.1
) under appropriate weak limits. This question was first raised by Burnett in
[
Bur89
]
. In this article, he considers sequences
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
of solutions to (
1.1
) on a given manifold
\mathcal{M}
such that the following convergence holds when
\lambda\to 0
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{g}_{0}\quad\text{uniformly on compact sets},

(1.2)

\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\partial\mathbf{g}_{0}\quad\text{weakly in $L^{2}_{loc}$},

where
\partial
denotes a partial derivative in any coordinates system on
\mathcal{M}
and where
\mathbf{g}_{0}
is a metric on
\mathcal{M}
. The question of interest here is the following: what matter model can the limit metric
\mathbf{g}_{0}
describe? More precisely, what are the possible effective stress-energy tensors
R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})-\frac{1}{2}R(\mathbf{g}_{0})(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\mu\nu}
? If the convergence of the derivatives
\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
towards
\partial\mathbf{g}_{0}
is strong then
\mathbf{g}_{0}
also solves (
1.1
), but if this convergence is only weak then products of derivatives of the metric in the Ricci tensor might produce backreaction, i.e a non-trivial effective stress-energy tensor. Burnett proposed a two-sided conjecture to answer the above questions:

•

The direct Burnett conjecture.
The effective stress-energy tensor is the one of a massless Vlasov field on
\mathcal{M}
, i.e there exists a density
f_{0}:\mathcal{M}\times T\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
(\mathbf{g}_{0},f_{0})
solves the massless Einstein-Vlasov system on
\mathcal{M}

\left\{\begin{aligned} R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})-\frac{1}{2}R(\mathbf{g}_{0})(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\mu\nu}&=\int_{\mathbf{g}_{0}(p,p)=0}f_{0}^{2}p_{\mu}p_{\nu},\\
p^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}f_{0}-p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\rho}_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{p^{\rho}}f_{0}&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

•

The reverse Burnett conjecture.
For every solution
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}_{0},f_{0})
of (
1.3
), there exists a sequence
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
of solutions of (
1.1
) on
\mathcal{M}
with the convergence properties (
1.2
).

Put together, these two parts of Burnett’s conjecture affirm that the closure of the set of solutions to (
1.1
) for the topology of the convergence (
1.2
) identifies with the set of solutions to (
1.3
).

The direct conjecture has been first considered in
[
GW11
]
and then proved in
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry, i.e when
\mathcal{M}
admits a spacelike Killing field, in
[
HL19
]
and
[
GdC21
]
, where the effective stress-energy tensor is identified by means of microlocal defect measures. The reverse conjecture, which is this article’s topic, is opposite in spirit to the direct one since it requires solving (
1.1
) with a given target
\mathbf{g}_{0}
. However, not only does it give a more complete and satisfying understanding of backreaction as a generic phenomenon, it is also highly connected to the direct conjecture. Indeed, the lack of strong convergence in (
1.2
) does not allow boundedness of the sequence
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
in
H^{2}_{loc}
so that the celebrated bounded
L^{2}
curvature conjecture proved in
[
KRS15
]
does not apply and the mere existence of sequences
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
of solutions to (
1.1
) displaying the pathological behaviour (
1.2
) is not guaranteed by the general theory. Solving the reverse conjecture precisely amounts to producing examples of such sequences and thus prevents the theorems on the direct conjecture to be empty.

The reverse conjecture has been proved first in
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry in
[
HL18
]
where the authors consider targets
\mathbf{g}_{0}
solving a discretized version of (
1.3
), namely the Einstein-null dusts system

\left\{\begin{aligned} R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})&=\sum_{\mathbf{A}}F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\partial_{\mu}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\nu}u_{\mathbf{A}},\\
\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{\mathbf{A}},\mathrm{d}u_{\mathbf{A}})&=0,\\
-2L_{\mathbf{A}}F_{\mathbf{A}}+(\Box_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}})F_{\mathbf{A}}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.4)

where
\mathbf{A}
runs through a finite set
\mathcal{A}
of arbitrary cardinal denoted
|\mathcal{A}|
in the sequel. In the setting of
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry, the same authors prove in
[
HL24
]
that one can take the limit
|\mathcal{A}|\to+\infty
and reach generic solutions to (
1.3
) as targets for backreaction. In both these works, the
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry plays an important role since it allows for the construction of an elliptic gauge, in which (
1.1
) reduces to a wave-map system coupled to semi-linear elliptic equations for the metric.

Outside of symmetry, the two sides of Burnett’s conjecture have been tackled in double null gauge in
[
LR20
]
, where the authors also shed new lights on the well-posedness theory for null dust shell and the formation of trapped surfaces. Their proof relies on a low-regularity result for (
1.1
) in double null gauge proved in
[
LR17
]
, where derivatives of the metric in the two null directions are allowed to be only in
L^{2}_{loc}
. Due to the structure of (
1.1
) in double null gauge, this has to be compensated by higher regularity in the angular directions. Therefore,
[
LR20
]
proves both sides of Burnett’s conjecture with limits
\mathbf{g}_{0}
solving (
1.4
) with
|\mathcal{A}|=2
and thus leaves open the question of whether there exists a setting for (
1.1
) capable of handling more than two null dusts. The present article’s main goal is to show that wave gauges provide such a setting. From this perspective, we obtain the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

[Rough version of the result from the point of view of Burnett’s conjecture]
The reverse Burnett conjecture holds true when
\mathbf{g}_{0}
is a generic solution of (
1.4
) in wave gauge with arbitrary
|\mathcal{A}|<+\infty
.

The intuitive reason behind the fact that wave gauges are good candidates to handle the superposition of more than two null dusts is that in these gauges the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
) rewrite as a system of quasi-linear wave equations which doesn’t single out any null direction, as opposed to double null gauges. We mention two interesting open problems:

•

As we will see, in this article we construct sequences
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
where the desired lack of strong convergence is purely due to oscillations. One of the strengths of
[
LR20
]
is to include concentration effects as the cause of weak convergence. It is an interesting open problem to prove Burnett’s reverse conjecture outside of symmetry with
|\mathcal{A}|\geq 3
and with concentration-based backreaction.

•

More importantly, this article completely leaves open the question of sending the number of null dusts to infinity and thus proving the reverse Burnett conjecture with the highest level of generality. In particular, our local existence result in generalised wave gauge is not uniform in
|\mathcal{A}|
and obtaining such uniformity seems to require ideas beyond the present work.

1.2
Multiphase geometric optics

The second motivation of our work is the multiphase geometric optics approximation for the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
). Geometric optics seeks a description of how waves propagate as solutions to a given system of PDEs. In general relativity, one can wonder how gravitational waves propagate as solutions to (
1.1
). As explained in depth in Chapter 35 of
[
MTW73
]
, one would also like to go beyond the linearized gravity setting which by definition cannot describe the energy of gravitational waves, a quadratic quantity by nature, and thus misses the global impact of gravitational waves on a background spacetime. The framework of geometric optics, as presented in
[
Rau12
]
or
[
Mé09
]
, provides such a setting, as we will now describe.

In
[
CB69
]
, Choquet-Bruhat is the first to construct WKB approximate solutions of (
1.1
). More precisely, she constructs a family of metrics of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{0}(x)+\lambda\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(x,\theta)_{|_{\theta=\frac{\phi(x)}{\lambda}}}+\lambda^{2}\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(x,\theta)_{|_{\theta=\frac{\phi(x)}{\lambda}}}

(1.5)

where the
\mathbf{g}^{(i)}
’s are periodic in the
\theta
variable and such that
R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})=O\left(\lambda\right)
. The typical geometric optics phenomena are recovered:
\phi
must solve the background eikonal equation and
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}
is transported along the rays. Less common is the fact that there should exist a scalar function
\tau>0
such that
R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})=\tau\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi
, thus making
[
CB69
]
one of the earliest examples of backreaction. As pointed out in
[
Mé09
]
, it remained to prove or disprove the stability of the geometric optics approximation, i.e to answer the following: does the approximate solution (
1.5
) stay close to an exact one on a uniform time scale in
\lambda
? A positive answer has been given in the articles
[
Tou23a
,
Tou23b
]
. As the approximate construction of
[
CB69
]
, these articles deal with the singlephase geometric optics approximation, when only one direction of oscillation is allowed. We extend these results to the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

[Rough version of the result from the point of view of geometric optics]
Under a strong coherence assumption, the multiphase geometric optics approximation is stable for (
1.1
).

Note that this result cannot follow from general results on multiphase geometric optics such as
[
JMR93
]
mainly because the hyperbolicity of (
1.1
) comes at the cost of a gauge choice, due to the invariance by diffeomorphism of the Ricci tensor. Here, the gauge is part of the geometric optics construction and in particular the metrics
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
will be such that
\Box_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}x^{\alpha}
, i.e the term defining the wave gauge, is oscillating and of order
\lambda
. Note that geometric optics for semi-linear gauge invariant equations have been studied in
[
Jea02
]
, here the situation is different since (
1.1
) are quasi-linear.

1.3
A discussion of transparency

The two motivations described above are obviously very much connected. Both are concerned with describing the non-linear interactions through (
1.1
) of small scale inhomogeneities in the metric, and geometric optics is used as a strategy to attack Burnett’s reverse conjecture. The non-linear structure of the Ricci tensor plays a central role in our proof, and we would like to bring to the reader’s attention a truly astounding aspect of (
1.1
), illustrated by Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

As it was already the case in the approximate construction of
[
CB69
]
, the transport equation along the rays of the optical function for the first profile in the WKB ansatz constructed in the present article is linear. At first glance, this is very surprising since the Einstein vacuum equations have a complicated non-linear structure. In the geometric optics literature, the fact that the transport equations for the first profile in a WKB ansatz turns out to be linear despite the waves interacting non-linearly is called transparency, see
[
JMR00
]
. Note that transparency can also be seen from Burnett’s conjecture’s perspective, since the transport equation for the density in (
1.3
) is linear. However, Burnett’s conjecture describes a truly non-linear effect since the effective stress-energy tensor, or alternatively the energy of the gravitational wave
\tau
in Choquet-Bruhat’s approximate construction, precisely originates in the quadratic self-interaction of derivatives of the metric. It is truly astounding that this interaction produces a global quadratic effect while being linearly propagated.

As pointed out in
[
Lan13
]
, transparency is directly linked to the null condition introduced in
[
Chr86
,
Kla86
]
for the study of global existence for small data for non-linear wave equations. Elaborating on
[
CB69
]
in her article
[
CB00
]
, Choquet-Bruhat uses geometric optics to show that (
1.1
) cannot truly satisfy the null condition, otherwise we would have
\tau=0
above or alternatively Burnett’s conjecture would reduce to an uninteresting statement about vacuum spacetimes necessarily approaching vacuum spacetimes. Later, Lindblad and Rodnianski introduced in
[
LR03
]
a weakened version of the null condition, rightly called the weak null condition. They show that (
1.1
) in wave gauge satisfy this condition and uses this to prove the stability of Minkowski in wave gauge in
[
LR10
]
, a result already proved in the seminal
[
CK93
]
. However, as noticed in Section 3.1.6 in
[
Tou23a
]
, there exist systems with the weak null condition but without the transparency property. The Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
) thus seem to be very much unique in the sense that both a linear and a non-linear behaviour can be simultaneously exhibited.

1.4
Notations and tools

In this section we introduce various notations and tools which will be used throughout the article.

Geometric notations.

•

Our construction takes place on the fixed manifold
\mathcal{M}\vcentcolon=[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, endowed with the standard coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
. We define
\Sigma_{t}\vcentcolon=\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
t\in[0,1]
. On
\mathcal{M}
we denote by
\mathbf{m}
the Minkowski metric while on each
\Sigma_{t}
the Euclidean metric is denoted by
e
. Greek indices will refer to the coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
and will thus run from 0 to 3. Latin indices will refer to the coordinates
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
and will thus run from 1 to 3. Repeated indices (with one up and one down) will be always summed over. If
r\geq 0
then
B_{r}
denotes the closed ball in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
centered at 0 and of radius
r
in the Euclidean metric, i.e
B_{r}=\{|x|\leq r\}
.

•

If
T
and
S
are symmetric 2-tensors and if
\mathbf{g}
is a Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{M}
then we define

\displaystyle|T\cdot S|_{\mathbf{g}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{g}^{\mu\nu}T_{\alpha\mu}S_{\beta\nu},

\displaystyle|T|_{\mathbf{g}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{g}^{\mu\nu}T_{\alpha\mu}T_{\beta\nu}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{g}}T

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}.

These notations have their natural 1-tensor equivalent:
|X\cdot Y|_{\mathbf{g}}=\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}X_{\alpha}Y_{\beta}
. We don’t distinguish between the covariant or contravariant forms of tensors, and if
T
is a general 2-tensor then its symmetric and anti-symmetric part are denoted

\displaystyle T_{(\alpha\beta)}

\displaystyle=T_{\alpha\beta}+T_{\beta\alpha},

\displaystyle T_{[\alpha\beta]}

\displaystyle=T_{\alpha\beta}-T_{\beta\alpha}.

All these notations extend naturally to tensors defined only on
\Sigma_{0}
endowed with a Riemannian metric.

•

For
f
a scalar function on
\mathcal{M}
,
\nabla f
will denote the Euclidean gradient of
f
or, alternatively and with a slight abuse of notations, any spatial derivatives
\partial_{i}f
while
\partial f
will denote any spacetime derivatives
\partial_{\alpha}f
. We associate to a Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}
on
\mathcal{M}
its wave operator
\Box_{\mathbf{g}}
which acts on scalar function as
\Box_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{g}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}f-\Gamma(\mathbf{g})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}f\right)
, where
\Gamma(\mathbf{g})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
denotes the usual Christoffel symbols of the metric
\mathbf{g}
. If
h
is a Riemannian metric the same definition in spacelike coordinates defined its Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta_{h}
. If
v
is a scalar function on
\Sigma_{0}
then we define its gradient with respect to the metric
h
by the vector field
\nabla_{h}v=h^{ij}\partial_{i}v\partial_{j}
.

Analytic tools.

•

On each slice
\Sigma_{t}
we define the usual Lebesgues and Sobolev spaces
L^{p}
and
W^{k,p}
with respect to the Euclidean element of integration. We also define the weighted Sobolev spaces
W^{k,p}_{\delta}
to be the completion of smooth and compactly supported functions on
\Sigma_{t}
for the norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{W^{k,p}_{\delta}}=\sum_{0\leq|m|\leq k}\left\|\left(1+|x|^{2}\right)^{\frac{\delta+|m|}{2}}\nabla^{m}f\right\|_{L^{p}}

(1.6)

with the standard special case
H^{k}_{\delta}\vcentcolon=W^{k,2}_{\delta}
and
L^{2}_{\delta}=H^{0}_{\delta}
. By replacing
L^{p}
in (
1.6
) by
L^{\infty}
we define the weighted Hölder spaces
C^{k}_{\delta}
. These norms are extended to tensors by summing over their components in the coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
.

•

From
[
CB09
]
, these spaces satisfy the continuous embeddings

\displaystyle W^{s_{1},p}_{\delta_{1}}\times W^{s_{2},p}_{\delta_{2}}\subset W^{s,p}_{\delta}

where
s\leq\min(s_{1},s_{2})
,
s<s_{1}+s_{2}-\frac{3}{p}
and
\delta<\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\frac{3}{p}
and

\displaystyle W^{s,p}_{\delta}\subset C^{m}_{\delta+\frac{3}{p}}

where
m<s-\frac{3}{p}
. From
[
CB09
]
we also get that
\Delta:H^{2}_{\delta}\longrightarrow L^{2}_{\delta+2}
is an isomorphism if
-\frac{3}{2}<\delta<-\frac{1}{2}
and where
\Delta
is the flat Laplacian, sometimes denoted
\Delta_{e}
.

•

Estimates with no mention of the time will always refer to estimates holding uniformly in time over
[0,1]
, i.e
\left\|f\right\|_{X}\leq C
will denote

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left\|f\right\|_{X(\Sigma_{t})}\leq C,

where
X
is any of the function spaces defined here.

High-frequency and schematic notations.

Since our construction is based on a geometric optics expansion of the metric, we will encounter objects defined or expressed via an expansion in terms of
\lambda
with oscillating coefficients, where by object we mean tensors of any types including scalar functions, vector fields, 1-forms and higher order tensors. We introduce some notations to describe and manipulate these expansions.

•

If an object
S
admits an expansion in terms of powers (non-negative or negative) of
\lambda
, we denote by
S^{(i)}
the coefficient of
\lambda^{i}
in this expansion. If
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\displaystyle S^{(\geq j)}=\sum_{k\geq j}\lambda^{k-j}S^{(k)},

so that

\displaystyle S=\sum_{k\leq j-1}\lambda^{k}S^{(k)}+\lambda^{j}S^{(\geq j)}.

•

A coefficient
S^{(i)}
in the expansion of an object
S
might oscillate at the frequency
\lambda^{-1}
, i.e be a linear combination of terms of the form

\displaystyle T\left(\frac{z}{\lambda}\right)S^{(i,T,z)}

for some
S^{(i,T,z)}
independent of
\lambda
(the notation
S^{(i,T,z)}
will not be used systematically, it just serves our purpose here),
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}
and
z
a phase, i.e a scalar function on
\mathcal{M}
.

•

In order to manipulate complicated non-linear expressions where high-frequency expansions might be differentiated and multiplied, we introduce a schematic notation:
\left\{f\right\}_{\alpha}
will denote any quantity with no oscillating function, depending on the index
\alpha
(this obviously extends to any number of indices) and of the form
f
. For instance the Christoffel symbol
\Gamma(\mathbf{g})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
of a metric
\mathbf{g}
might be rewritten
\left\{\mathbf{g}^{-1}\partial\mathbf{g}\right\}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
.

•

We extend this notation to include undescribed oscillations:
\left\{f\right\}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{osc}}
will denote any quantity of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 0\\
z\text{ scalar function}\\
T\in\{1,\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{i}T\left(\frac{z}{\lambda}\right)\left\{f\right\}_{\alpha}.

Some important tensor operators.

We conclude this section by introducing several operators acting on symmetric 2-tensors and related to the action of the Ricci tensor on oscillating tensors.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
v
be a scalar function on
\mathcal{M}
and
S
a symmetric 2-tensor.

(i)

We define the polarization tensor of
S
with respect to
v
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\left[S\middle|v\right]_{\alpha}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\mu\nu}S_{\alpha\mu}\partial_{\nu}v-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}S\partial_{\alpha}v.

(ii)

We define the operator
\mathcal{P}_{v}
by setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{v}(S)_{\alpha\beta}\vcentcolon=-\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)S_{\alpha\beta}+\partial_{(\alpha}v\mathscr{P}\left[S\middle|v\right]_{\beta)}.

Note that if
\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}u)=0
, then in any null frame
\left(L,\underline{L},e^{(1)},e^{(2)}\right)
associated to
u
(see the next section for the definition of a null frame) we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\left[S\middle|u\right]_{L}

\displaystyle=-S_{LL},

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\left[S\middle|u\right]_{e^{(\mathbf{i})}}

\displaystyle=-S_{Le^{(\mathbf{i})}},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\left[S\middle|u\right]_{\underline{L}}

\displaystyle=-S_{e^{(1)}e^{(1)}}-S_{e^{(2)}e^{(2)}}.

(1.9)

Remark 1.1
.

In our previous work
[
Tou23a
]
,
\mathscr{P}\left[S\middle|v\right]
was denoted by
\mathrm{Pol}(S)
(there was no need to include the phase function in the notation since only one was considered). This latter notation can be misleading since a 2-tensor satisfying
\mathrm{Pol}(S)=0
is physically polarized, while the notation suggests that it has ’zero’ polarization. We thank Thibault Damour for this remark, according to which we changed our notation.

The operator
\mathcal{P}_{v}
will play a very important role in our construction in the case
\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)\neq 0
since it will describe the leading term in the Ricci tensor when acting on a tensor oscillating with the phase function
v
. The next lemma shows how one can invert this operator modulo its kernel (which we won’t need). Its straightforward proof is left to the reader, it relies on the identity

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\left[\partial_{(\cdot}vQ_{\cdot)}\middle|v\right]=\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)Q.

(1.10)

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
v
be a scalar function on
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)\neq 0
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ran}\mathcal{P}_{v}=\left\{A\text{ symmetric 2-tensor}~\middle|~\mathscr{P}\left[A\middle|v\right]=0\right\}.

Moreover, if
\mathscr{P}\left[A\middle|v\right]=0
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{v}\left(-\frac{1}{\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)}A\right)=A.

1.5
The background null dusts spacetime

We assume that a regular solution of the Einstein-null dusts system is given on the manifold
\mathcal{M}
. More precisely, we consider given on
\mathcal{M}
a Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}_{0}
and two families of scalar functions
\left(u_{\mathbf{A}}\right)_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}}
and
\left(F_{\mathbf{A}}\right)_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}}
, where the index
\mathbf{A}
runs through a finite set
\mathcal{A}
, solving the Einstein-null dusts system

\left\{\begin{aligned} R_{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})&=\sum_{\mathbf{A}}F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\partial_{\mu}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\nu}u_{\mathbf{A}},\\
\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{\mathbf{A}},\mathrm{d}u_{\mathbf{A}})&=0,\\
-2L_{\mathbf{A}}F_{\mathbf{A}}+(\Box_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}})F_{\mathbf{A}}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.11)

where
L_{\mathbf{A}}\vcentcolon=-\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\beta}
is the spacetime gradient and is assumed to be future-directed. Thanks to the eikonal equation in (
1.11
),
L_{\mathbf{A}}
is null and geodesic i.e
\mathbf{g}_{0}(L_{\mathbf{A}},L_{\mathbf{A}})=0
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}L_{\mathbf{A}}=0,

(1.12)

where
\mathbf{D}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection associated to
\mathbf{g}_{0}
.

Gauge condition.

We assume that
\mathbf{g}_{0}
satisfies the wave gauge condition

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\mu\nu}\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{0})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}=0,

(1.13)

in the coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
of
\mathcal{M}
. This allows us to rewrite the first equation of (
1.11
) as

\displaystyle-\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\alpha\beta}+P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{0})(\partial\mathbf{g}_{0},\partial\mathbf{g}_{0})=2\sum_{\mathbf{A}}F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\partial_{\mu}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\nu}u_{\mathbf{A}}.

(1.14)

where

\displaystyle P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{0})(\partial\mathbf{g}_{0},\partial\mathbf{g}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\mu\rho}\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\nu\sigma}\bigg(\partial_{(\alpha}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\beta)\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\partial_{\rho}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\alpha\nu}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\beta\mu}+\partial_{\rho}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\sigma\alpha}\partial_{\mu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\nu\beta}\bigg).

Regularity and decay.

We will measure the regularity by an integer
N\geq 10
and the decay by a real constant
\delta
satisfying
-\frac{3}{2}<\delta<-\frac{1}{2}
. We also introduce a smallness constant
\varepsilon>0
.

•

We assume that the metric
\mathbf{g}_{0}
is close to Minkowski in the following sense

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{g}_{0}-\mathbf{m}\right\|_{H^{N+1}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{g}_{0}\right\|_{H^{N}_{\delta+1}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\mathbf{g}_{0}\right\|_{H^{N-1}_{\delta+2}}\leq\varepsilon,

(1.15)

where
\mathbf{m}
is the Minkowski metric on
\mathcal{M}
.

•

We assume that there exist constant non-zero vector fields
\mathfrak{z}_{\mathbf{A}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that for all
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u_{\mathbf{A}}-\mathfrak{z}_{\mathbf{A}}\right\|_{H^{N}_{\delta+1}}\leq\varepsilon.

(1.16)

We associate to each
u_{\mathbf{A}}
a transport operator acting on tensors of all type

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{A}}\vcentcolon=-2\mathbf{D}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}+\Box_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}.

•

We assume that all densities
F_{\mathbf{A}|_{\Sigma_{0}}}
are initially supported in a ball
B_{R}\subset\Sigma_{0}
for some fixed
R>0
. The transport equation in (
1.11
) then implies that each
F_{\mathbf{A}}
is supported in
J^{+}_{0}\left(B_{R}\right)
, i.e the causal future associated to
\mathbf{g}_{0}
. Thanks to (
1.15
)-(
1.16
) there exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{supp}}>0
such that
J^{+}_{0}\left(B_{R}\right)\subset\left\{(t,x)\in\mathcal{M}~\middle|~|x|\leq C_{\mathrm{supp}}R\right\}
. We also assume that for all
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|F_{\mathbf{A}}\right\|_{H^{N}}\leq\varepsilon.

(1.17)

Strong coherence.

We introduce some sets of phases. First, the null harmonics that will appear in the metric
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{k}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{ku_{\mathbf{A}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2}\cup\mathcal{N}_{3}.

Second, the mixed harmonics that will appear in the metric
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}\text{ and }\pm\in\{-1,+1\}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm 2u_{\mathbf{B}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}\text{ and }\pm\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm_{1}u_{\mathbf{B}}\pm_{2}u_{\mathbf{C}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\text{ all distincts and }\pm_{1},\pm_{2}\in\{-1,+1\}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{I}_{2}\cup\mathcal{I}_{3}.

We also define
\mathcal{W}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{I}
. Finally, the mixed harmonics that will appear using the contracted Bianchi identities in Section
5.2
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{4}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm 3u_{\mathbf{B}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}\text{ and }\pm\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm_{1}u_{\mathbf{B}}\pm_{2}2u_{\mathbf{C}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\text{ all distincts and }\pm_{1},\pm_{2}\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm_{1}u_{\mathbf{B}}\pm_{2}u_{\mathbf{C}}\pm_{3}u_{\mathbf{D}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}\text{ all distincts and }\pm_{1},\pm_{2},\pm_{3}\in\{-1,+1\}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{5}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm 4u_{\mathbf{B}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}\text{ and }\pm\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{3u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm 2u_{\mathbf{B}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}\text{ and }\pm\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm_{1}2u_{\mathbf{B}}\pm_{2}2u_{\mathbf{C}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\text{ all distincts and }\pm_{1},\pm_{2}\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm_{1}u_{\mathbf{B}}\pm_{2}3u_{\mathbf{C}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\text{ all distincts and }\pm_{1},\pm_{2}\in\{-1,+1\}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cup\left\{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm_{1}u_{\mathbf{B}}\pm_{2}u_{\mathbf{C}}\pm_{3}2u_{\mathbf{D}}~\middle|~\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}\text{ all distincts and }\pm_{1},\pm_{2},\pm_{3}\in\{-1,+1\}\right\},

We also define
\mathcal{Z}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{I}_{4}\cup\mathcal{I}_{5}
. Our geometric optics construction will rely on the following strong coherence assumption: there exists a constant
c_{\mathrm{coherence}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\min_{v\in\mathcal{I}}|\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)|\geq c_{\mathrm{coherence}}.

(1.18)

We also assume that there exists a constant
c_{\mathrm{spatial}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left(\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla z|\right)>c_{\mathrm{spatial}}.

(1.19)

Remark 1.2
.

The assumptions (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) can be proved to hold under the assumption that the phases
u_{\mathbf{A}}
are initially angularly separated, as was first noticed in
[
HL18
]
. More precisely, if there exists
\eta\in(0,1)
such that for
\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\nabla u_{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\nabla u_{\mathbf{B}}}{|\nabla u_{\mathbf{A}}||\nabla u_{\mathbf{B}}|}\leq 1-\eta

on
\Sigma_{0}
and if (
1.19
) holds on
\Sigma_{0}
, then Huneau and Luk show that if
\varepsilon
is small enough then there exists positive constants
c_{\mathbf{A}}
such that if we replace the phases
u_{\mathbf{A}}
by
u_{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=c_{\mathbf{A}}u_{\mathbf{A}}
then (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) hold for some constants
c_{\mathrm{coherence}}
and
c_{\mathrm{spatial}}
(note that
(\mathbf{g}_{0},c_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}F_{\mathbf{A}},u_{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime})
would still solve (
1.11
)). Note that strictly speaking
[
HL18
]
consider less mixed phases in
\mathcal{Z}
than the present article but their argument extends easily. Finally, note that ensuring angular separation and (
1.19
) on
\Sigma_{0}
can be done by choosing some angularly separated
\mathfrak{z}_{\mathbf{A}}
(which is a generic choice) and then choosing
\varepsilon
small enough in (
1.16
). In particular, the assumptions (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) are generic.

We mention a important consequence of (
1.19
): together with a stationary phase argument we can show that for all
T:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
smooth,
2\pi
-periodic with
\int_{0}^{2\pi}T=0
and for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
the sequence of functions
\left(T\left(\frac{z}{\lambda}\right)\right)_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
converges weakly to 0 in
L^{2}(K)
when
\lambda\to 0
and where
K
is any compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Initial data.

The background spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}_{0},u_{\mathbf{A}},F_{\mathbf{A}})
induces an initial data set

\left(\Sigma_{0},g_{0},k_{0},u_{\mathbf{A}|_{\Sigma_{0}}},F_{\mathbf{A}|_{\Sigma_{0}}}\right)

on
\Sigma_{0}
solving the null dusts constraint equations

\displaystyle R(g_{0})-|k_{0}|^{2}_{g_{0}}+(\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}k_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\mathbf{A}}(\partial_{t}u_{\mathbf{A}})^{2}F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2},

(1.20)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}k_{0})_{i}-\partial_{i}\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}k_{0}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{t}u_{\mathbf{A}}F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\partial_{i}u_{\mathbf{A}},

(1.21)

and satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|g_{0}-e\right\|_{H^{N+1}_{\delta}}+\left\|k_{0}\right\|_{H^{N}_{\delta+1}}+\lesssim\varepsilon.

(1.22)

To simplify the resolution of the constraint equations on
\Sigma_{0}
and the definition of initial data for the geometric optics hierarchy of equations, we make the generic assumption that
\partial_{t}
is the future-directed unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
\mathbf{g}_{0}
. This implies that the second fundamental form
k_{0}
of
\Sigma_{0}
in
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}_{0})
is given in coordinates by
(k_{0})_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(g_{0})_{ij}
and that the spatial components of (
1.13
) rewrite

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{0\ell}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{ij}\left(\partial_{i}(g_{0})_{j\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\ell}(g_{0})_{ij}\right),

(1.23)

on
\Sigma_{0}
. This simplifying assumption also has consequences regarding optical functions
u_{\mathbf{A}}
. Since they solve the eikonal equations and that their spacetime gradient is assumed to be future-directed we have on
\Sigma_{0}
and for all
\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{\mathbf{A}}=|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}.

(1.24)

On
\Sigma_{0}
we define the following vector fields

\displaystyle N_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}}{|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}},

(1.25)

\displaystyle N^{(\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{\nabla_{g_{0}}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}})}{|\nabla_{g_{0}}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}})|_{g_{0}}},

where
\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}
in the second definition and where we used (
1.15
) and (
1.19
) to divide by
|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}
or
|\nabla_{g_{0}}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}})|_{g_{0}}
. Note that (
1.24
) implies that

\displaystyle L_{\mathbf{A}|_{\Sigma_{0}}}

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}(\partial_{t}-N_{\mathbf{A}}).

Moreover, if we project the geodesic equation (
1.12
) onto
\Sigma_{0}
, i.e compute
\mathbf{g}_{0}(\mathbf{D}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}L_{\mathbf{A}},\partial_{\ell})
, we obtain

\displaystyle-N_{\mathbf{A}}(N_{\mathbf{A}})_{\ell}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}N_{\mathbf{A}}^{a}\partial_{\ell}(g_{0})_{ka}+\frac{1}{|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}}\partial_{\ell}|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}=\frac{1}{|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}}(N_{\mathbf{A}})_{\ell}N_{\mathbf{A}}|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}.

(1.26)

This identity will be used in the proof of Lemma
3.4
.

Null frames.

To each optical function
u_{\mathbf{A}}
we associate a null frame
\left(L_{\mathbf{A}},\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)
on
\mathcal{M}
, which thus satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{0}(L_{\mathbf{A}},L_{\mathbf{A}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{0}(\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}},\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}})=\mathbf{g}_{0}\left(L_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(\mathbf{i})}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)=\mathbf{g}_{0}\left(\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(\mathbf{i})}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)=0

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{0}(L_{\mathbf{A}},\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}})

\displaystyle=-2,\qquad\mathbf{g}_{0}\left(e^{(\mathbf{i})}_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(\mathbf{j})}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)=\delta_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{j}}.

Moreover, we ask that
\left(e^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)
is an orthonormal frame for
\mathbf{g}_{0}
of
TP_{\mathbf{A},t,u}
where

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{A},t,u}=\Sigma_{t}\cap\left\{(\tau,x)\in\mathcal{M}~\middle|~u_{\mathbf{A}}(\tau,x)=u\right\},

which, thanks to (
1.16
), has the topology of a plane in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. More details on the definition of such null frames can be found in
[
Sze18
]
. Finally, note that
\left(N_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}},e^{(2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)
is an orthonormal frame for
g_{0}
of
T\Sigma_{0}
and that
\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}|_{\Sigma_{0}}}=|\nabla_{g_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}}|_{g_{0}}(\partial_{t}+N_{\mathbf{A}})
.

Remark 1.3
.

In this article, we don’t prove that such a background spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}_{0},u_{\mathbf{A}},F_{\mathbf{A}})
can be constructed, this follows from
[
CBF06
]
adapted to the null dusts case.

1.6
Statement of the result

The following theorem is the main result of this article.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}_{0},u_{\mathbf{A}},F_{\mathbf{A}})
be a solution of the Einstein-null dusts system as described in Section
1.5
. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(N,\delta,R)>0
such that if
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
then there exists a family
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
of solutions to the Einstein vacuum equations on
\mathcal{M}
of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{0}+\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}+\lambda^{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{w}{\lambda}\right)F^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}+\widetilde{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\right).

(1.27)

Moreover,

(i)

the metrics
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
solve (
1.1
) in generalised wave gauge and in the coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0\quad\text{uniformly on compact sets},

(1.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\left(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup 0\quad\text{weakly in $L^{2}_{loc}$},

when
\lambda\to 0
,

(ii)

the tensors
F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
F^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}
are supported in
J^{+}_{0}\left(B_{R}\right)
and
F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
satisfies

\displaystyle-2\mathbf{D}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}+(\Box_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}})F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.29)

\displaystyle\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}

\displaystyle=8F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2},

(1.30)

(iii)

there exists
C=C(N,\delta,R)>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right\|_{H^{N}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon,

(1.31)

\displaystyle\left\|F^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,5\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\partial\nabla^{r}F^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon,

(1.32)

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\partial\nabla^{r}\widetilde{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon.

(1.33)

Some comments are in order.

•

The reverse Burnett conjecture.
The estimates stated in Theorem
1.3
are consistent with Burnett’s weak convergence as in (
1.2
). Indeed if
K
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
then (
1.32
)-(
1.33
) and the standard Sobolev embedding
H^{2}\subset L^{\infty}
imply

\displaystyle\left\|F^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}+\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\lesssim\lambda^{-1},

and thus the convergence of
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
towards
\mathbf{g}_{0}
uniformly on compact sets. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\partial(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathbf{g}_{0})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\partial u_{\mathbf{A}}F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda\left(\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\partial F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}\left(\frac{w}{\lambda}\right)\partial wF^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda^{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{w}{\lambda}\right)\partial F^{(2,w,T)}_{\lambda}+\partial\widetilde{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\right).

Thanks to (
1.19
) and (
1.31
),
\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\partial u_{\mathbf{A}}F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
converges weakly to 0 in
L^{2}_{loc}
and (
1.32
)-(
1.33
) again implies that the remaining terms in
\partial(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathbf{g}_{0})
converge actually strongly to 0 in
L^{2}_{loc}
. Therefore, Theorem
1.3
indeed implies Theorem
1.1
, where the genericity mentioned in the latter refers to the assumptions (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) (see Remark
1.2
).

•

Parametrization of the
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
.
For a given background spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}_{0},u_{\mathbf{A}},F_{\mathbf{A}})
as described in Section
1.5
, we actually construct several families
(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
as in Theorem
1.3
. These families are parametrized by a set of seeds living on the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
and defined in Definition
3.1
below. Each seed is parametrized by two real numbers, which correspond to the coefficients of two possible polarizations for the leading oscillating term in (
1.27
), as in the linearized gravity setting in TT gauge (see Remark
3.1
).

•

The generalised wave gauge.
The exact expression of the generalised wave gauge term
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
is somehow irrelevant since we don’t really prescribe what it should contain but rather what it should not contain, namely first order derivatives of some of the metric components solving (or coupled with) a wave equation. A novelty compared to
[
Tou23a
]
is that we also use this gauge term to control the interaction of waves propagating in different directions, see the discussion on
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
in Section
2.2.3
. Overall, the gauge term helps us recover true hyperbolicity and ellipticity of the Ricci tensor.

•

The constraint equations.
As in every resolution of (
1.1
) from a spacelike hypersurface, one should solve first the constraint equations on the initial hypersurface. In our previous work on the singlephase case, we separated the resolution of the constraint equations on
\Sigma_{0}
from the resolution of (
1.1
) on
\mathcal{M}
, resulting in the two articles
[
Tou23a
]
and
[
Tou23b
]
. For the multiphase case, we decided to associate the elliptic and hyperbolic aspects of the construction in one self-consistent article. In particular, we want to highlight the surprising connections between the elliptic and hyperbolic procedures due to the oscillatory aspect of the metric. Some of these connections were already present in the singlephase case, but some are specific to the multiphase case, see the discussion after Proposition
3.1
.

•

Gauge-independent transparency.
We conclude these comments by insisting on the astounding structures in the Einstein vacuum equations leading in particular to (
1.29
)-(
1.30
). These relations satisfied by the first profiles in the ansatz (
1.27
) for
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
illustrate perfectly the discussion of Section
1.3
: the self-interaction of gravitational waves produces a quadratic macroscopic effect (
1.30
) while each wave is linearly transported (
1.29
). Moreover the interaction of gravitational waves propagating in different null directions don’t produce a macroscopic effect, in particular since the first mixed harmonics
v\in\mathcal{I}
only appear at order
\lambda^{2}
in (
1.27
). Finally, note that both the linear propagation and non-linear backreaction are phenomena that are independent of the choice of coordinates since (
1.29
) and (
1.11
) are tensorial equations.

The rest of this article is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

2
The high-frequency ansatz

In this section we present the high-frequency ansatz for the metric
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
. Its expression is given in Section
2.1
, its Ricci tensor is computed in Section
2.2
and finally in Section
2.3
a hierarchy of equations and conditions is deduced from the requirement that
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
solves the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
).

2.1
Expansion of the metric

For
\lambda>0
, we consider the following metric

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{0}+\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}+\lambda^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}+F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)+\cos\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right)

(2.1)

\displaystyle+\lambda^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B},\pm}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}+\lambda^{2}\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle+\lambda^{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}+\lambda^{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}.

In this expression,

•

F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}
, are symmetric 2-tensors which don’t depend on
\lambda
and we already assume
F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}=F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}}
,

•

the remainder
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
and the amplitude
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}
are symmetric 2-tensors and do depend on
\lambda
. The higher order symmetric 2-tensors
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
will depend on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}
and thus on
\lambda
.

The ansatz (
2.1
) differs from the singlephase ansatz in
[
Tou23a
]
only because of the terms
F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
. They will crucially be used to control the creation of mixed harmonics, i.e terms oscillating with phases
v\in\mathcal{I}
, at order
\lambda^{0}
and
\lambda^{1}
in the Ricci tensor of
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
respectively.

2.2
Expansion of the Ricci tensor

In this section, we compute the Ricci tensor of the metric
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
. Since we want
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
to solve (
1.1
) in generalised wave gauge we use the following decomposition of the Ricci tensor of Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}
:

\displaystyle 2R_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g})=-\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\beta}+P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g})(\partial\mathbf{g},\partial\mathbf{g})+\mathbf{g}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}+H^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\beta},

(2.2)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbf{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu},

(2.3)

\displaystyle H^{\rho}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbf{g}^{\mu\nu}\Gamma(\mathbf{g})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu},

(2.4)

\displaystyle P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g})(\partial\mathbf{g},\partial\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbf{g}^{\mu\rho}\mathbf{g}^{\nu\sigma}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}\mathbf{g}_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta)\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{g}_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\beta}\mathbf{g}_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\rho}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\nu}\partial_{\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\mu}+\partial_{\rho}\mathbf{g}_{\sigma\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\nu\beta}\right).

(2.5)

We expand the wave part
\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\alpha\beta}
in Section
2.2.1
, the quadratic non-linearity
P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})(\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda},\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})
in Section
2.2.2
and the gauge term
H^{\rho}
in Section
2.2.3
. We combine the results in Section
2.2.4
to obtain the expansion of
R_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})
. The computations will be performed under the following assumption on
F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\left[F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

(2.6)

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{L}_{\mathbf{A}}}

\displaystyle=0.

Note that thanks to (
1.7
)-(
1.9
) the assumption (
2.6
) is equivalent to
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}=0
and
(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}\alpha}=0
. They are consistent with the singlephase case of
[
Tou23a
]
and the approximate construction of
[
CB69
]
. More precisely, the first assumption in (
2.6
) ensures that
R_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})=O\left(1\right)
. The second assumption is convenient to simplify the computations and is not necessary. They will be proved to be consistent with the hierarchy derived from (
1.1
), as in
[
Tou23a
]
.

Remark 2.1
.

We introduce two conventions on the use of the bracket schematic notations introduced in Section
1.4
:

•

In a bracket we will never write down the quantities depending on the background spacetime introduced in Section
1.5
, since we are not solving for it. This includes metric or inverse metric coefficients, phases
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, vector fields from the null frames.

•

In a bracket we will never specify the indices referring to the set
\mathcal{A}
. For instance,
\left\{F^{(1)}\right\}
simply denotes any of the
\left\{F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right\}
for
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}
. Similarly
\left\{\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}\right\}
denotes any of the
\left\{\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}\right\}
for
u\in\mathcal{N}
and
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}
(same for
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}
).

These conventions also apply to the notation
\left\{\cdot\right\}^{\mathrm{osc}}
and will be used throughout the article.

2.2.1
The wave part

In this section we expand the wave term in (
2.2
), i.e
\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\alpha\beta}
. The result is contained in the next proposition, whose proof is postponed to Appendix
A.1
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Under the assumptions (
2.6
), the wave part of the Ricci tensor of
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
admits the expansion

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(W^{(0,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B},\pm}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}}{\lambda}\right)(W^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.7)

\displaystyle+\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(W^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(W^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)(W^{(1)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\lambda^{2}W^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}.

The terms of order 0 are given by

\displaystyle(W^{(0,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=2L_{\mathbf{A}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}-(\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}})(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta},

(2.8)

\displaystyle(W^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}),\mathrm{d}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}))(F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}\left((F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{B}}L_{\mathbf{B}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}\right).

The terms of order 1 are given by

\displaystyle(W^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=(-2L_{\mathbf{A}}+\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}})\left((\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+(F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}\right)+(\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\{\left(1+F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}\right)F^{(1)}+\partial^{2}F^{(1)}\right\}_{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle(W^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-2(-2L_{\mathbf{A}}+\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}u_{\mathbf{A}})(F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+\left\{\left(F^{(2,1)}+\partial F^{(1)}+F^{(1)}\right)F^{(1)}\right\}_{\alpha\beta},

(2.11)

\displaystyle(W^{(1)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.12)

\displaystyle\quad+\Big\{\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(2,\pm)}+\left(\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}+F^{(1)}+\partial F^{(1)}\right)F^{(1)}\Big\}_{\alpha\beta}.

The higher order terms are given by

\displaystyle W^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda})_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.13)

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T})_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big\{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\left(\partial^{\leq 1}\left(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}+\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}+\mathfrak{F}\right)+\partial^{\leq 2}\left(F^{(1)}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}\right)\right)\Big\}_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{osc}}.

We draw the reader’s attention on several structural facts from Lemma
2.1
:

1.

The main term produced by the wave part of the Ricci is a transport term along the rays: at order
\lambda^{0}
we see the transport of
F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
by the vector field
L_{\mathbf{A}}
and at order
\lambda^{1}
we see the transport of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}
by
L_{\mathbf{A}}
again. By imposing transport equations for these tensors we will be able to absorb most terms at these orders in the Ricci tensor. Therefore, the admissible terms at order
\lambda^{0}
oscillate like
\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
and the admissible terms at order
\lambda^{1}
oscillate like
\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
and
\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
. We already see in (
2.7
) the presence at order
\lambda^{1}
of forbidden term such as
(F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}
oscillating like
\cos\left(\frac{3u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
, we will deal with it differently.

2.

At order
\lambda^{2}
, we obviously recover the wave operator acting on the remainder
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
. Note how we made a difference in (
2.13
) between second order derivatives of the various tensors in (
2.1
). Second order derivatives of
F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}
are considered error terms and put in the brackets, while second order derivatives of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
are carefully conserved and in particular the wave operator structure will crucially be used to prove well-posedness.

3.

Opposite to the terms oscillating in null directions, the wave operator acts ’elliptically’ on terms oscillating with phase
v\in\mathcal{I}
since
\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)\neq 0
whenever
v\in\mathcal{I}
(see (
1.18
)). This explains the presence of
F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}
in (
2.9
) and of
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
in (
2.12
).

2.2.2
The quadratic non-linearity

In this section we expand the quadratic non-linearity in (
2.2
), i.e
P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})(\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda},\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})
. The result is contained in the next proposition, whose proof is postponed to Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Under the assumptions (
2.6
), the quadratic part of the Ricci tensor of
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
admits the expansion

\displaystyle P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})(\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda},\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{0})(\partial\mathbf{g}_{0},\partial\mathbf{g}_{0})-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathbf{A}}+\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(P^{(0,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.14)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(P^{(0,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B},\pm}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}}{\lambda}\right)(P^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(P^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(P^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u(\hat{P}^{(1)}_{u,T})_{\beta)}+\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)(P^{(1)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\lambda^{2}P^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}.

The terms of order 0 are given by

\displaystyle(P^{(0,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{0})^{\mu}_{(\alpha\rho}\partial^{\rho}u_{\mathbf{A}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)\mu}-(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\mu\nu}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\partial_{\mu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\beta)\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta)}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\mu\nu}\right),

(2.15)

\displaystyle(P^{(0,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathbf{A}},

(2.16)

\displaystyle(P^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\pm\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{B}}\sigma}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\beta)}^{\sigma}\pm\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{B}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}\sigma}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)}^{\sigma}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\pm\frac{1}{2}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\beta)}u_{\mathbf{B}}\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\cdot F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}}\right|_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}\pm(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{(\alpha L_{\mathbf{B}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\beta)L_{\mathbf{A}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\mp\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{\mathbf{A}},\mathrm{d}u_{\mathbf{B}})(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{(\alpha}^{\nu}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\nu\beta)}\bigg).

The terms of order 1 are given by

\displaystyle(P^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=2\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{0})^{\mu}_{(\alpha\rho}\partial^{\rho}u_{\mathbf{A}}\left((\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)\mu}+(F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)\mu}\right)

(2.18)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\{\partial F^{(1)}+F^{(1)}F^{(2,\pm)}+F^{(1)}+(F^{(1)})^{3}\right\}_{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle(P^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-4\Gamma(\mathbf{g}_{0})^{\mu}_{(\alpha\rho}\partial^{\rho}u_{\mathbf{A}}(F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)\mu}+\left\{F^{(1)}\partial F^{(1)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}\right\}_{\alpha\beta},

(2.19)

\displaystyle(\hat{P}^{(1)}_{u,T})_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(1+F^{(1)}\right)\left((F^{(1)})^{2}+\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}\right)\right\}_{\beta},

(2.20)

\displaystyle(P^{(1)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\Big\{F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}+(F^{(1)})^{3}+F^{(1)}\left(\partial F^{(1)}+\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}\right)\Big\}_{\alpha\beta}.

(2.21)

The terms of order 2 are simply of the form

\displaystyle P^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}\right)^{(\geq 2)}\right\}_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{osc}}.

(2.22)

We draw the reader’s attention on several structural facts from Lemma
2.2
:

1.

It is clear from the resonant term in (
2.14
) that the quadratic part of the Ricci tensor is responsible for backreaction, i.e the fact that the Ricci tensor of the background spacetime
\mathbf{g}_{0}
cannot vanish and needs to absorb the sum of
\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathbf{A}}
, which in particular makes clear the null dusts structure of backreaction.

2.

Following the first comment after Lemma
2.1
, we see the presence at order
\lambda^{0}
of a forbidden term due to
P^{(0,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}
. This term is the high-high counterpart of backreaction and its particular null dust tensorial structure
\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathbf{A}}
given in (
2.16
) will be crucially used. Similarly, the forbidden harmonics
3u_{\mathbf{A}}
is present at order
\lambda^{1}
with a particular tensorial structure
\partial_{(\alpha}u(\hat{P}^{(1)}_{u,T})_{\beta)}
(recall that
\mathcal{N}
includes the third harmonics
3u_{\mathbf{A}}
).

2.2.3
The gauge part

In this section we expand the gauge term
H^{\rho}
which appears differentiated in (
2.2
). In the usual wave gauge, it is simply set to 0. In our case, we will set to 0 only some problematic terms in
H^{\rho}
which we regroup and call
\Upsilon^{\rho}
. The terms that we will put in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
will serve two main purposes: first recover hyperbolicity and obtain a well-posed final system and second simplify the control of the mixed harmonics at order
\lambda^{1}
. As a secondary purpose it will allow
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}
not to depend on the remainder
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
. For now, we simply give the definition of
\Upsilon^{\rho}
:

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\rho}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\Bigg(\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda})_{\mu\nu}+\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\mu\nu}\right)\Bigg)

(2.23)

\displaystyle+\lambda\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T})_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}v(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}v(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\Bigg(\partial_{\mu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\mu\nu}-\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{\mathbf{A}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}u_{\mathbf{A}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\mu\nu}\right)\Bigg).

The gauge term is expanded in the next proposition, whose proof is postponed to Appendix
A.3
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Under the assumptions (
2.6
), the gauge term
H^{\rho}
satisfies

\displaystyle H^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\mathring{H}^{\rho}+\lambda^{2}\Upsilon^{\rho}

where
\mathring{H}^{\rho}
admits the expansion

\displaystyle\mathring{H}^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(H^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\rho}+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(H^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\rho}\right)+\lambda\sum_{\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B},\pm}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}}{\lambda}\right)(H^{(1,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})^{\rho}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda^{2}\left((H^{(2)})^{\rho}+\Upsilon^{\rho}\right)+\lambda^{3}(H^{(\geq 3)})^{\rho}.

The terms of order 1 are given by

\displaystyle(H^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathscr{P}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}+F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\sigma}+\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\mu\nu}\right)

(2.24)

\displaystyle\quad-(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\mu\nu}\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{\mu}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\mu\nu}\right),

\displaystyle(H^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\rho}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathscr{P}\left[F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\partial^{\rho}u_{\mathbf{A}}\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}},

(2.25)

\displaystyle(H^{(1,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})^{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathscr{P}\left[F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}\right]_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{8}\partial^{\rho}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}})\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\cdot F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}}\right|_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})^{\nu}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\nu}^{\rho}\mp\frac{1}{4}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\nu}_{L_{\mathbf{B}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\nu}^{\rho}.

The terms of order 2 are given by

\displaystyle(H^{(2)})^{\rho}

(2.27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathscr{P}\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}\middle|u\right]_{\sigma}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{1,\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\Big\{\partial F^{(2,1)}+\partial F^{(2,2)}

\displaystyle+\left(\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}+\partial F^{(1)}\right)\left(1+F^{(1)}\right)\Big\}^{\rho}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{1,\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\Big\{\partial F^{(2,\pm)}

\displaystyle+\left(\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}+\partial F^{(1)}\right)\left(1+F^{(1)}\right)\Big\}^{\rho}.

The terms of order 3 are of the form

\displaystyle(H^{(\geq 3)})^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\left((\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})^{(\geq 2)}+(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1})^{(\geq 1)}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1})^{(\geq 1)}\right)

(2.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left(\partial F^{(1)}+\mathfrak{F}+\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(2,1)}+\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(2,2)}+\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(2,\pm)}+\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}\right)\Big\}^{\rho,\mathrm{osc}}.

We draw the reader’s attention on several structural facts from Lemma
2.3
:

1.

The gauge term
H^{\rho}
contains first order derivatives of the metric. Some appear in
\mathring{H}^{\rho}
and some appear in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
, i.e are considered as problematic terms from the well-posedness point of view. In particular, first order derivatives of
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
,
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
have been included in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
, see the first three lines of (
2.23
). By doing so, the second order derivatives of these tensors due to derivatives of
H^{\rho}
in the Ricci tensor won’t appear in the final system, where only the wave terms from (
2.13
) will be allowed (recall the second comment after Lemma
2.1
).

2.

At order
\lambda^{1}
and
\lambda^{2}
, we see the polarization tensors of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}
appearing as leading terms, where we recall that polarization tensors have been defined in Definition
1.1
. Note that the polarization tensor of
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
should also appear in (
2.27
). However, we included the polarization tensor of
\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T}
in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
in order to remove it from the final hierarchy, see the fourth line in (
2.23
). The reason to do so will be explained after Proposition
2.1
.

2.2.4
The Ricci tensor

In this section, we put together the results of Lemmas
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
and obtain the final expression of
R_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})
in the next proposition, whose proof is postponed to Appendix
A.4
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Under the assumptions (
2.6
), the Ricci tensor of
\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}
admits the expansion

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda^{2}R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}.

The term
R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\left(F_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}-\frac{1}{8}\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}\right)\partial_{\mu}u_{\mathbf{A}}\partial_{\nu}u_{\mathbf{A}}+(R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{null}})_{\alpha\beta}+(R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{mixed}})_{\alpha\beta},

(2.29)

where

\displaystyle 2(R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{null}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\bigg((\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{A}}F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}-\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\mathscr{P}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\beta)}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\mathscr{P}\left[F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\beta)}+(Q^{(0)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)}\right)\bigg)

(2.30)

\displaystyle\quad-4\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{\mathbf{A}}\left(\mathscr{P}\left[F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\beta)}+\frac{3}{32}\partial_{\beta)}u_{\mathbf{A}}\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\right|^{2}_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}\right),

\displaystyle 2(R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{mixed}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B},\pm}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathcal{P}_{u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}}}\left(F^{(2,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}\right)_{\alpha\beta}+(\mathcal{I}^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}\right),

(2.31)

with

\displaystyle(Q^{(0)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbf{g}_{0}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\mu\nu}\right),

(2.32)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{I}^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-\frac{1}{4}\left((F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{B}}L_{\mathbf{B}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}\right)+(P^{(0,\pm)}_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.33)

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{(\alpha}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}})\bigg(-\frac{1}{8}\partial_{\beta)}(u_{\mathbf{A}}\pm u_{\mathbf{B}})\left|F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\cdot F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}}\right|_{\mathbf{g}_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\frac{1}{4}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})^{\nu}_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\nu\beta)}\mp\frac{1}{4}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\nu}_{L_{\mathbf{B}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{B}})_{\nu\beta)}\bigg).

The term
R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=(R^{(1)}_{\mathrm{null}})_{\alpha\beta}+(R^{(1)}_{\mathrm{mixed}})_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{g}_{0})_{\rho(\alpha}(\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho})^{(-1)},

(2.34)

where

\displaystyle 2(R^{(1)}_{\mathrm{null}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\bigg((\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+(\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{A}}F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}+(\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\mathscr{P}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}}+F^{(2,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\beta)}+(\tilde{R}^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\bigg((\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{A}}F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\mathscr{P}\left[F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}\middle|u_{\mathbf{A}}\right]_{\beta)}+(\tilde{R}^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\bigg(2\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)-\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\bigg)(F^{(2,2)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{L_{\mathbf{A}}L_{\mathbf{A}}}(F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u\left(-\mathscr{P}\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T}\middle|u\right]_{\beta)}+(\tilde{R}^{(1,h,T,u)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\beta)}\right),

\displaystyle 2(R^{(1)}_{\mathrm{mixed}})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\left(\mathbf{g}_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}v)(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}+(\tilde{R}^{(1,e,T,v)}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(2.36)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(1,1)}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}\right)F^{(1)}+\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(1)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}\right\},

(2.37)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(1,2)}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(F^{(2,1)}+\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(1)}\right)F^{(1)}\right\}_{\alpha\beta},

(2.38)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(1,h,T,u)}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\partial F^{(2,1)}+\partial F^{(2,2)}

(2.39)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left(\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}+\partial F^{(1)}\right)\left(1+F^{(1)}\right)\Big\}_{\beta)},

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(1,e,T,v)}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}

(2.40)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left(\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,1)}+F^{(2,2)}+F^{(2,\pm)}+(F^{(1)})^{2}+\partial^{\leq 1}F^{(1)}\right)\left(1+F^{(1)}\right)\Big\}.

The term
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle 2R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda})_{\alpha\beta}-\sum_{\mathbf{A}}\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbf{A}})_{\alpha\beta}

(2.41)

\displaystyle-\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{N}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}_{u,T})_{\alpha\beta}-\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{I}\\
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}\end{subarray}}T\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}_{v,T})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\tilde{R}^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\left(H^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\alpha\beta}+(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}\right)^{(\geq 2)}.

where

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\left(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}\partial\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}\right)^{(\geq 2)}+\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\Big(\partial^{\leq 1}\mathfrak{g}^{(3,h)}+\partial^{\leq 1}\mathfrak{g}^{(3,e)}+\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(1)}+\partial^{\leq 1}\mathfrak{F}

(2.42)

\displaystyle+\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(2,1)}+\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(2,2)}+\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(2,\pm)}\Big)\Big\}_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{osc}}.

In Section
2.3
and
2.4
below we will precisely state the hierarchy of equations and relations that will ensure
R_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda})=0
. Before that, we briefly discuss the mechanisms at stake, following the comments made after Lemmas
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
:

1.

Admissible terms.
The admissible terms at order
\lambda^{0}
and
\lambda^{1}
, i.e the
\sin\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
terms in (
2.30
) and the
\cos\left(\frac{u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
and
\sin\left(\frac{2u_{\mathbf{A}}}{\lambda}\right)
terms in (
2.35
) can be set to zero by imposing transport equations for
F^{(1)}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
